This completes the proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:collar-extension" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:collar-extension">21</a>.

<a id="stmt-collar-scaling-interface"></a>
<div id="collar:scaling-interface" class="remark">

*Remark 24* (Scaling and the time-dependent interface). If the physical collar radius is $b$, write the normalized metric as $g_b=b^{-2}\Phi_b^*g$, where $\Phi_b(x)=bx$ in the chosen coordinates. Covariant Ricci components of $g_b$ are $b^2$ times the physical Cartesian components; intrinsic Ricci magnitudes and their scaled derivatives consequently gain the same $b^2$ factor. The residual term in physical units is $b^{-2}\theta_R/R^2=\theta_R/(Rb)^2$. For a differentiable family on a fixed material collar, the bounded derivative of the analytic extension gives $$|\partial_t\hat g|_{0,r}\le CB_1(t)r^{-q},\qquad
 |\mathop{\mathrm{Ric}}(\hat g)|_{0,r}\le Cb^{-2}m(t)r^{-q-2},$$ when the collar velocity is bounded by $B_1(t)$ in the required fixed $H^k$ norm and $m(t)$ bounds Equation <a href="#eq:collar-residual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:collar-residual">[eq:collar-residual]</a> in collar units. The physical volume factor is $b^4$, so the tail pairing of these two tensors is bounded by $Cb^2m(t)B_1(t)\int_1^\infty r^{1-2q}\,dr$, which is finite precisely for $q>1$. No derivative of the comparison map $F$, the far exterior coordinates, the weights, or $R$ occurs. All constructions use only the finite orders specified above. For $k=30$, scaled Sobolev embedding in dimension four gives $C^{27}$ metric bounds and $C^{25}$ Ricci bounds from $H^{30}$ and $H^{28}$, respectively. These supply the finite $C^{20}$ metric and $C^{10}$ Ricci bounds used in Section <a href="#sec:static-input" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:static-input">2</a>.

</div>

<a id="sec:contradiction"></a>
### Functional change and the contradiction

<a id="final:energy-recall"></a>
We apply the preceding results to the buffered intervals of Proposition <a href="#prop:buffered-intervals" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:buffered-intervals">12</a>. The interval is $I=[-\theta,1+\theta]$ in its rescaled time coordinate. The central interval $[0,1]$ has original duration $d_i\to0$, whereas the full buffered interval has original duration $(1+2\theta)d_i$. Put $\epsilon_i=\sqrt{d_i}$. We suppress the index $i$ when no limit is being taken. Thus $|R|\le C\epsilon_i^2$, the reference root length $a=a_i$ tends to zero, and the root scale defined in Proposition <a href="#prop:root-scale" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:root-scale">8</a> is comparable to $a$ throughout $I$. Its values at $0$ and $1$ differ by a fixed factor. The centers $x_t$, cutoff $\eta$, and energy $K$ are those of Equation <a href="#eq:root-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:root-energy">[eq:root-energy]</a>; in particular $$\label{final:energy-recall}
 a^4 K^2=\int_I\eta(t)^2
       \int_{B_t(x_t,N_1a)}|\mathop{\mathrm{Ric}}|^2\,d\mu_{g(t)}\,dt,
 \qquad \min_{[0,1]}\eta>0.$$ The maximal function $H_*$ may use a larger fixed ball than the one in Equation <a href="#final:energy-recall" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="final:energy-recall">[final:energy-recall]</a>, as permitted in Equation <a href="#eq:maximal-ricci" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:maximal-ricci">[eq:maximal-ricci]</a>.

First fix the small collar tolerance. The estimates below hold for every sufficiently large fixed dyadic number $N\gg N_1$, with $b=Na$. All limits $i\to\infty$ are taken with $N$ fixed. The constants multiplying $N^{-2\alpha}K^2$ will be independent of large $N$; constants multiplying a quantity denoted $o_i(1)$ may depend on $N$. At the final absorption step we choose one such $N$ large enough to absorb that explicit loss, then take the sequence limit. No subsequent limit in $N$ is needed.

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### Material collars and time patches

<a id="stmt-final-collar-position"></a>
<div id="final:collar-position" class="lemma">

**Lemma 25** (Position of a controlled material collar). *Choose sufficiently small $\sigma_0>0$ and sufficiently large fixed $N$. At a time near $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\eta$, choose an exterior annular chart at scale $b$ with domain, in collar units, $\{1/8<|x|<16\}/\Gamma$, retaining slightly larger boundary margins when necessary. Keep its map into the material manifold and its group fixed. For all sufficiently late $i$, throughout any interval on which its metric stays within $\sigma_0$ of the flat metric through the required fixed derivative order, the following assertions hold. Every closed shortened collar lies at distance comparable to $b$ from the current center $x_t$; its middle bands cover the corresponding shortened distance annuli. The component on the designated inner side of a middle sphere contains $B_t(x_t,N_1a)$ and is contained in $B_t(x_t,Cb)$, with fixed constants.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-final-collar-position"><summary>Proof of stmt-final-collar-position</summary>

*Proof.* The initial chart has these properties by the exterior annular convergence in Proposition <a href="#prop:exterior-ricci" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:exterior-ricci">18</a>. In particular, a middle sphere separates: every path from a sufficiently small ball to outside a sufficiently large comparable ball crosses the chart’s radial band and hence the sphere. Its two sides belong to different components. A connected two-sided embedded closed hypersurface in a connected manifold has at most two complementary components: every component reaches a tubular neighborhood of the hypersurface, and each of its two connected sides meets only one component. Thus the designated inner component is unambiguous and is fixed as a material subset while the chart is kept fixed.

We justify the uniform positional assertion; closeness of the metric in a chart alone would not imply it. If the assertion failed for arbitrarily small tolerances and arbitrarily large $N$, choose a countersequence with $\sigma_0\to0$, $N\to\infty$, and indices as late as necessary for each choice. The chart lies in the material neighborhood of the same isolated point of the limiting space $Z$. At scale $b$, based at an interior chart point, Theorem <a href="#thm:degeneration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:degeneration">6</a> gives a complete flat quotient limit: a nonflat limit at this scale, which is much larger than $a$, would contradict the outermost-scale property in Proposition <a href="#prop:root-scale" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:root-scale">8</a>. On each compact subannulus the controlled chart supplies curvature bounds on balls with a definite margin. Indeed a path leaving the chart must first traverse that margin in its controlled metric. Smooth compactness therefore extracts the chart maps locally into the regular part of the flat limit; metric and connection transformation formulas give convergence of the maps and a limiting local flat isometry.

The groups can be fixed after passage to a subsequence. Their orders are bounded by noncollapse, there are finitely many abstract groups of bounded order, and their orthogonal representations have convergent subsequences. Close orthogonal representations of a fixed finite group are conjugate: averaging an approximate intertwiner over the group makes it an exact invertible intertwiner, and its polar factor is orthogonal and still intertwines. The source group is nontrivial and acts freely on the sphere. The target flat limit is a global Euclidean quotient with at most one exceptional point, by Theorem <a href="#thm:degeneration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:degeneration">6</a>. Lift the local isometry to Euclidean covers of regular annuli. It is the restriction of a rigid motion $x\mapsto Ax+c$, since its differential is parallel. Equivariance conjugates the source group into the target group. Its translation part fixes $c$ under that nontrivial free spherical action, so $c=0$ after centering the target quotient. In particular the target group is nontrivial and the image is a complete centered open annulus.

There is no multiplicity in that image. Otherwise two distinct interior source points would approach one target point. In the approximations they could then be joined by a path of length $o(b)$. A path contained in the chart cannot do this by its controlled metric, and a path leaving it costs a fixed positive multiple of $b$ before reaching the boundary. Both alternatives are impossible. The target tip is represented by points $z_i$ with $l(z_i)/b_i\to0$. It must lie at distance $o(b_i)$ from the current cluster center. Otherwise, on a subsequence, put $h_i=d_t(z_i,x_t)\ge cb_i$; the case $h_i/b_i\to\infty$ is allowed. The chart’s material points approach the same point of $Z$ by their initial positioning and uniform convergence of the original distances. The same holds for $z_i$, which is within $O(b_i)$ of an interior chart point, and for $x_t$. Thus the original length corresponding to $h_i$ tends to zero. At this separation scale, $$\frac{l(z_i)}{h_i}\longrightarrow0,\qquad
 \frac{l(x_t)}{h_i}\le C\frac{a_i}{h_i}
                    \le\frac{C}{cN_i}\longrightarrow0.$$ Here the maximizing-center detection in Proposition <a href="#prop:root-scale" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:root-scale">8</a> gives $l(x_t)\asymp a_i$; this placement countersequence has $N_i\to\infty$. The separation-scale limit has two genuine tips at distance one. It is therefore nonflat, since a flat limit has at most one tip. Its scale satisfies $h_i/a_i\ge cN_i\to\infty$, contradicting the outermost-scale property in Proposition <a href="#prop:root-scale" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:root-scale">8</a>.

These facts imply coverage in the approximations. A point whose limit belongs to the interior image has distance $o(b)$ from an image point with a fixed chart margin; a path realizing that distance cannot leave the image, so the point itself lies in it. Boundary positions follow by approaching the boundary from interior points and using the controlled lengths. Consequently all shortened radial bands have their claimed position and coverage, to any prescribed fixed accuracy. Crossing such bands shows that the geometric inward component contains the small ball and is contained in the larger $Cb$ ball. To identify it with the designated material component, fix a regular material point $p$ at positive $d_T$-distance from this isolated cluster. At the chart’s initial time $p$ belongs to the outward component. Uniform convergence of original distances keeps $p$ outside the current $Cb$ ball at every time under consideration. Thus it still belongs to the geometric outward component. The embedded sphere and its two complementary material components were kept fixed, so the other component remains the designated inward one. This contradicts the countersequence and proves the lemma with fixed choices of tolerance and $N$, followed by sufficiently late indices. ◻

</details>

<a id="final:collar-weights"></a>
At any individual time in such a patch, complete exterior coordinates can be chosen to agree with the fixed chart on $C_1=\{1\le|x|\le2\}/\Gamma$. Here and below an outward fixed rescaling of the chosen middle collar is harmless. To see the agreement, take the single neck coordinates from Equation <a href="#eq:neck-decay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:neck-decay">[eq:neck-decay]</a> out to a sufficiently small fixed radius $r_*<r_0$ in interval units. On the roomy overlap their transition with the fixed chart has metric distortion $O(\sigma_0+N^{-\mu})$ and controlled connection difference. Lifting the overlap, integration of the connection transformation makes this transition close to a rigid equivariant map, through all needed orders. Its linear part can be adjusted to be orthogonal by polar factorization. The group and whole-band identifications are those proved in Lemma <a href="#final:collar-position" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="final:collar-position">25</a>. After that rigid alignment the maps are close to identity, so a radial cutoff interpolation on a shorter overlap has invertible derivative and patches the coordinate systems. This is precisely the overlap construction of the neck charts, now performed only in a regular fixed-width band. Decreasing $\mu>0$ if needed, the shell weights in Theorem <a href="#thm:collar-extension" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:collar-extension">21</a> can consequently be chosen as $$\label{final:collar-weights}
 \theta_r\le C\bigl[(\sigma_0+N^{-\mu})r^{-\mu}
                    +\delta_i(rb)^\mu\bigr],
 \qquad 1\le r\le4R,\qquad Rb\asymp r_*.$$ They include the collar norm times $r^{-q}$ after decreasing $\mu<q$. The dyadic sum of their first term is small with the fixed choices; the second sum tends to zero because $Rb\asymp r_*$. These coordinates outside $C_1$ are used only to estimate the error. They are not differentiated in time.

Let $B_1(t)$ be an upper bound for $|\mathop{\mathrm{Ric}}|_{k+4,b}$ on a fixed ample band of true distance radii $[c'b,C'b]$ around $x_t$, in interval units. Suprema over all admissible center choices can be used to make this bound independent of a choice of chart. It is measurable and locally bounded for every $i$. Define an increasing absolutely continuous time coordinate by <a id="final:time-coordinate"></a>$$\label{final:time-coordinate}
 u(t)=\int_{t_*}^{t}(1+B_1(s))\,ds .$$ Start each chart with a tolerance strictly better than $\sigma_0$, as is possible by first taking $N$ large. As long as the chart remains within $\sigma_0$, Lemma <a href="#final:collar-position" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="final:collar-position">25</a> places its entire closed domain in the band defining $B_1$. The flow equation $\partial_tg=-2\mathop{\mathrm{Ric}}$, together with conversion between covariant and coordinate derivatives in that controlled chart, then gives $$\|g(t)-g(s)\|_{C^{k+3}(\text{fixed collar})}
       \le C\int_s^t B_1(v)\,dv .$$ A first-exit argument proves validity for a fixed sufficiently small $u$-width. This holds in both time directions, using the absolute value of the integral.

<a id="final:partition"></a>
On an interval with room around $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\eta$, choose regularly spaced centers in the $u$ coordinate, subordinate smooth nonnegative bumps of that fixed width, and normalize their sum. Pulling them back gives an absolutely continuous partition $\{\chi_j\}$ with $$\label{final:partition}
 \sum_j\chi_j=1\quad\hbox{near }\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\eta,\qquad
 \sum_j|\chi_j'(t)|\le C(1+B_1(t))\quad\hbox{a.e.}$$ Its overlap is bounded by a fixed number. For each $i$ it is finite on the compact interval in use; no bound on the number of members uniform in $i$ is asserted or needed.

On patch $j$, retain the true metric on the material inner component and apply Theorem <a href="#thm:collar-extension" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:collar-extension">21</a> in its fixed collar. Interpolate as in that theorem. Denote the resulting metric on the fixed manifold with one end by $\hat g_j(t)$, and its functional in interval length units by $E_j(t)$, using Equation <a href="#eq:energy-functional" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:energy-functional">[eq:energy-functional]</a>. Different patches are allowed to have different extensions and different functional values.

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
#### The lower bound for functional variation

<a id="stmt-final-collar-bound"></a>
<div id="final:collar-bound" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:collar-time-bound"></a>
**Lemma 26** (The time-integrated collar error). *The residual in Equation <a href="#eq:collar-residual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:collar-residual">[eq:collar-residual]</a> for every active patch is bounded by a common nonnegative function $m(t)$ such that $$\label{eq:collar-time-bound}
 \sup_{\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\eta}m=o_i(1),\qquad
 b^{-2}\|\eta m\|_{L^2(I)}+\|\eta B_1\|_{L^2(I)}
 \le C\bigl[(a/b)^{2+\alpha}K+\rho_i\bigr],
 \quad \rho_i\longrightarrow0.$$ The leading constant is independent of large $N$; $\rho_i$ may depend on fixed $N$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-final-collar-bound"><summary>Proof of stmt-final-collar-bound</summary>

*Proof.* Use the wider true bands in every supremum in the residual. In collar units the uncut regularity estimate Equation <a href="#eq:ricci-improvement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ricci-improvement">[eq:ricci-improvement]</a> bounds Ricci and its scaled derivatives on the shell of radius $r$ by $C\epsilon_i b/r$. The dyadic sum of $r^{-1}$ is bounded, the zero-order terms carry the summable weights $\theta_r$, and $\theta_R/R^2\to0$. Thus $m=o_i(1)$ uniformly. This argument uses the uncut estimate and is valid even where $\eta$ is small.

For the integrated assertion multiply the residual by $\eta$ and convert a collar-unit Ricci magnitude to interval units by $b^{-2}$. Writing $\rho=br$ for a shell’s interval radius, its positive-order terms are bounded in time $L^2$ by $V(\rho)$ from Proposition <a href="#prop:exterior-ricci" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:exterior-ricci">18</a>; its zero-order term is bounded by $\theta_r A(\rho)$. The initial collar term is $A(b)$ up to fixed-width changes. Consequently it suffices to sum $$A(b)+\sum_{r}\bigl[V(br)+\theta_r A(br)\bigr]
             +C\frac{\theta_R}{(Rb)^2}.$$ The leading energy terms sum to $C(a/b)^{2+\alpha}K$. The errors in $V$ are summable in both directions: $$\sum_{br\in[cb,Cr_*]}
       \bigl[(a/(br))^\alpha+(br)^\alpha\bigr]
       \le C\bigl[(a/b)^\alpha+r_*^\alpha\bigr].$$ The errors in $A$ are summed only with $\theta_r$, whose sum is uniformly bounded. In particular no constant error is multiplied by the number of shells. Finally $\theta_R/(Rb)^2\to0$, because $Rb\asymp r_*>0$ and Equation <a href="#final:collar-weights" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="final:collar-weights">[final:collar-weights]</a> makes $\theta_R\to0$. The same bounds for the fixed ample band give the assertion for $B_1$. Minkowski’s inequality proves Equation <a href="#eq:collar-time-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:collar-time-bound">[eq:collar-time-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-final-variation"></a>
<div id="final:variation" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:variation-lower"></a>
**Proposition 27** (Positive variation supplied by the true core). *For the preceding extensions and partition, $$\label{eq:variation-lower}
 \sum_j\int_I\eta^2\chi_j E_j'\,dt
 \ge 2a^4K^2-a^4\bigl[CN^{-2\alpha}K^2
                              +o_i(1)(K+1)^2\bigr].$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-final-variation"><summary>Proof of stmt-final-variation</summary>

*Proof.* The analytic collar map and its bounded derivative make $E_j$ differentiable on its patch. The available fixed Sobolev orders give all spatial derivatives required by the first-variation formula in Equation <a href="#ell:first-variation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ell:first-variation">[ell:first-variation]</a>. On the retained true part, $$\left\langle \tfrac12 Rg-\mathop{\mathrm{Ric}},\partial_tg\right\rangle
           =2|\mathop{\mathrm{Ric}}|^2-R^2.$$ That part contains $B_t(x_t,N_1a)$ by Lemma <a href="#final:collar-position" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="final:collar-position">25</a>, and its volume is at most $Cb^4$. The scalar-square loss integrated in time is therefore at most $C\epsilon_i^4b^4=o_i(a^4)$ for fixed $N$.

On the interpolated collar and attached end, use collar radius $r\ge1$. Equation <a href="#eq:collar-extension" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:collar-extension">[eq:collar-extension]</a>, Sobolev embedding, and the derivative bound for the extension map imply $$|\mathop{\mathrm{Ric}}(\hat g_j)|\le Cb^{-2}m(t)r^{-2-q},\qquad
 |\partial_t\hat g_j|_{\hat g_j}\le CB_1(t)r^{-q},\qquad
 d\mu_{\hat g_j}\le Cb^4r^3\,dr\,d\omega.$$ There is no extra power of $b$ in the second estimate: normalizing the collar metric multiplies its covariant tensor by $b^{-2}$, whereas pulling back the original covariant Ricci tensor contributes $b^2$ relative to its interval-unit magnitude. The two factors cancel. Since $|R|\le2|\mathop{\mathrm{Ric}}|$ in dimension four, the absolute value of the tail contribution to $E_j'$ is bounded by <a id="final:tail-variation"></a>$$\label{final:tail-variation}
 Cb^2m(t)B_1(t)\int_1^\infty r^{1-2q}\,dr
       \le Cb^2m(t)B_1(t).$$ This is the specific use of $q>1$ in the time-variation estimate. Summing against $\chi_j$ costs no multiplicity, since $\sum_j\chi_j=1$. By Equation <a href="#eq:collar-time-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:collar-time-bound">[eq:collar-time-bound]</a> and Cauchy–Schwarz the integrated tail loss is at most $$Cb^2\|\eta m\|_2\|\eta B_1\|_2
 \le Cb^4\bigl[(a/b)^{2+\alpha}K+\rho_i\bigr]^2.$$ Its leading factor is exactly <a id="final:tail-scaling"></a>$$\label{final:tail-scaling}
 b^4(a/b)^{4+2\alpha}=a^4N^{-2\alpha}.$$ For fixed $N$ the remaining terms are $o_i(a^4)(K+1)^2$. The positive core term sums to at least $2a^4K^2$ by Equation <a href="#final:energy-recall" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="final:energy-recall">[final:energy-recall]</a>. This proves the proposition. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
#### Applying the static inequality to every time patch

The lower variation bound is expressed in terms of the true root energy. We now obtain the opposing upper bound. The only required uniformity is over the ratios of functional value to weighted Ricci error: a new fixed tree may be extracted from each proposed failure of that uniformity.

<a id="stmt-final-functional-smallness"></a>
<div id="final:functional-smallness" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:functional-littleoh"></a>
**Proposition 28** (Uniform functional smallness). *For fixed $N$ there are numbers $\zeta_i\to0$ such that, at every time in every active patch, $$\label{eq:functional-littleoh}
 |\eta(t)E_j(t)|\le
 \zeta_i a^4\bigl[H_*(t)+1+a^{-2}\eta(t)m(t)\bigr].$$ Here $H_*$ uses a fixed enlargement depending on $N$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-final-functional-smallness"><summary>Proof of stmt-final-functional-smallness</summary>

*Proof.* We first verify exactly which static hypotheses hold after an arbitrary choice of indices, times $t_i$ with $\eta(t_i)>0$, and active patches $j_i$. Rescale their extensions by the length $a_i$. Theorem <a href="#thm:degeneration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:degeneration">6</a> and Proposition <a href="#prop:root-scale" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:root-scale">8</a> extract one finite rooted tree, with the outermost nonflat entry represented at unit scale. Every smaller concentration is contained inside the collar: if a concentration escaped to distance much greater than $a$, the distance-scale limit with the root would have two genuine tips and hence a larger nonflat entry. For large fixed $N$, the replacement collar and all its overlaps are uniformly regular and leave a detecting nonflat root patch in the retained body.

For clarity, the hypotheses of Theorem <a href="#thm:tree-inequality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:tree-inequality">4</a> are supplied as follows. The original compact pieces and joining regions have the sequential identifications from Theorem <a href="#thm:degeneration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:degeneration">6</a>. On an edge with parent scale $A$ and child scale $Ae^{-2L}$, put $$\tau_p=\log(A/\rho),\qquad
 \tau_c=\log(\rho/(Ae^{-2L})),\qquad \tau_p+\tau_c=2L.$$ The physical radius is $\rho=Ae^{-\tau_p}$ on the parent half. On the child half, $\tau_c\le L$, so $\rho=Ae^{-2L+\tau_c}\le Ae^{-\tau_c}$. Every parent scale is bounded by a fixed multiple of $a$. Consequently $\rho\le C_Nae^{-D}$ on both halves, where $D=\min(\tau_p,\tau_c)$ up to fixed end-coordinate offsets. Equation <a href="#eq:neck-decay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:neck-decay">[eq:neck-decay]</a> bounds the metric error by a constant times $e^{-\mu\tau_p}+e^{-\mu\tau_c}$. On the parent half $\tau_p\le L$ this is at most $2e^{-\mu\tau_p}$, and on the child half it is at most $2e^{-\mu\tau_c}$. Thus these are exactly the one-sided half-neck weights of the static theorem, with a possibly smaller fixed positive exponent. The joined groups are nontrivial and are identified by those same annular charts.

On the new root exterior, Equation <a href="#eq:collar-extension" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:collar-extension">[eq:collar-extension]</a> gives uniform $O(r^{-q})$ control with $1<q<2$ and sufficiently many scaled derivatives. Expressing it in root units changes its constant by a factor depending on $N$, which is fixed. Local Sobolev compactness extracts convergence through the finite differentiability order needed by the static theorem. The limit is Ricci-flat on the attached portion because $m(t_i)\to0$. Local harmonic coordinates and elliptic regularity make this Ricci-flat limit smooth on its regular part; the weighted finite-order bounds are retained in the original end coordinates. The collar embeddings themselves extract on smaller regular overlaps: metric and connection bounds control their transitions with the interior convergence charts. Adjusting the latter by these limiting transitions identifies the two limits on the overlaps. Hence the substitution changes only a regular portion and the unjoined end of the root vertex. It does not alter a joining end or remove the retained nonflat patch. This gives one fixed limiting Ricci-flat root with the required decay $q>1$, while every other vertex is the one furnished by the true slice convergence. A fixed tree is obtained only on this extracted sequence; no tree through time is being chosen.

Let $M_i$ be a bound for the weighted, scale-neutralized Ricci error in Equation <a href="#eq:tree-ricci-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tree-ricci-error">[eq:tree-ricci-error]</a> on this sequence. Choose its internal weight exponent smaller than both the half-neck metric exponent and the exponent in Equation <a href="#eq:interior-weighted" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:interior-weighted">[eq:interior-weighted]</a>. We can take $M_i\to0$ without using $\eta$. Indeed on a true piece of physical radius $\rho$ in interval units the uncut bound is $$\rho^2|\mathop{\mathrm{Ric}}|_{j,\rho}\le C\epsilon_i\rho.$$ On compact pieces $\rho\le C_Na$. At logarithmic depth $D$ in a joining neck one has $\rho\le C_Nae^{-D}$, so multiplication by a sufficiently small positive exponential weight still leaves a quantity tending to zero. The substituted region contributes at most $C_Nm(t_i)$ with the required root-end decay. These statements also hold for all derivatives required in the definition of $M_i$.

<a id="final:weighted-error"></a>
Independently, Equation <a href="#eq:interior-weighted" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:interior-weighted">[eq:interior-weighted]</a> supplies the stronger bound relative to the energy: $$\label{final:weighted-error}
 \eta(t_i)M_i
 \le C_{\mathrm{ext}}a_i^2\bigl[H_*(t_i)+\epsilon_i\bigr]
                         +C_{\mathrm{ext}}\eta(t_i)m(t_i).$$ Here the constant may depend on this extraction and on fixed $N$. To check the power of $a$, multiply that equation’s $(\rho/a)^2|\eta\mathop{\mathrm{Ric}}|_{j,\rho}$ by $a^2$; the result is the scale-neutralized tensor $\rho^2|\eta\mathop{\mathrm{Ric}}|_{j,\rho}$ required in Equation <a href="#eq:tree-ricci-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tree-ricci-error">[eq:tree-ricci-error]</a>. Compact retained regions are handled by shorter regular patches, and the energy ball for $H_*$ is enlarged once to contain their short-time comparisons. On the collar and new end the error is $C_{\mathrm{ext}}\eta m$ by Equation <a href="#eq:collar-extension" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:collar-extension">[eq:collar-extension]</a>. This proves Equation <a href="#final:weighted-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="final:weighted-error">[final:weighted-error]</a> for the same $M_i$ obtained as the maximum of these weighted errors. Its uncut smallness and its cutoff energy bound are separate assertions; neither was obtained by division by $\eta$.

<a id="final:ratio-test"></a>
Write $E_i^{\mathrm{root}}$ for the functional in root units. Theorem <a href="#thm:tree-inequality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:tree-inequality">4</a> now gives $|E_i^{\mathrm{root}}|=o(M_i)$ on this extraction, and the scaling law of Equation <a href="#eq:energy-functional" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:energy-functional">[eq:energy-functional]</a> gives $E_{j_i}(t_i)=a_i^2E_i^{\mathrm{root}}$. If $M_i=0$, the zero-error scaling argument in that theorem gives $E_i^{\mathrm{root}}=0$ directly. Otherwise, using Equation <a href="#final:weighted-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="final:weighted-error">[final:weighted-error]</a> and $\epsilon_i\le1$, $$\label{final:ratio-test}
 \frac{|\eta(t_i)E_{j_i}(t_i)|}
 {a_i^4[H_*(t_i)+1+a_i^{-2}\eta(t_i)m(t_i)]}
 \le C_{\mathrm{ext}}\frac{|E_i^{\mathrm{root}}|}{M_i}
 \longrightarrow0.$$

If Equation <a href="#eq:functional-littleoh" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functional-littleoh">[eq:functional-littleoh]</a> failed uniformly, its nonnegative ratio would be bounded below by some fixed number along a sequence of active patches and times. Times with $\eta=0$ have zero numerator, so that sequence has $\eta>0$. The extraction just performed yields Equation <a href="#final:ratio-test" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="final:ratio-test">[final:ratio-test]</a> on a subsequence, a contradiction. The extraction-dependent constant is fixed on that subsequence and multiplies a quantity tending to zero. This proves the uniform assertion without a uniform tree, a uniform analytic family, or a uniform power-law exponent. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-final-energy-vanishes"></a>
<div id="final:energy-vanishes" class="corollary">

**Corollary 29**. *Along the buffered intervals, $K_i\to0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-final-energy-vanishes"><summary>Proof of stmt-final-energy-vanishes</summary>

<a id="final:ibp-bound"></a>
*Proof.* Each product $\eta^2\chi_j$ has compact support in the patch where $E_j$ is defined. Integration by parts there gives $$\sum_j\int_I\eta^2\chi_j E_j'\,dt
 =-\sum_j\int_I
       (2\eta\eta'\chi_j+\eta^2\chi_j')E_j\,dt.$$ Absolute continuity of the partition is sufficient for this identity. No values of different $E_j$ are equated at a switch. By Equations <a href="#final:partition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="final:partition">[final:partition]</a> and <a href="#eq:functional-littleoh" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:functional-littleoh">[eq:functional-littleoh]</a>, the absolute value is at most $$\label{final:ibp-bound}
 C\zeta_i a^4\int_I
 [H_*+1+a^{-2}\eta m]\,[|\eta'|+\eta(1+B_1)]\,dt.$$ For fixed $N$, Equations <a href="#eq:maximal-ricci" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:maximal-ricci">[eq:maximal-ricci]</a> and <a href="#eq:collar-time-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:collar-time-bound">[eq:collar-time-bound]</a> show $$\|H_*+1+a^{-2}\eta m\|_2\le C_N(K+1),\qquad
 \||\eta'|+\eta(1+B_1)\|_2\le C_N(K+1).$$ For the first inequality use $a^{-2}=N^2b^{-2}$; fixed powers of $N$ are allowed here. Cauchy–Schwarz in Equation <a href="#final:ibp-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="final:ibp-bound">[final:ibp-bound]</a> therefore bounds it by $o_i(a^4)(K+1)^2$.

Choose and now fix $N$ so large that the leading constant in Equation <a href="#eq:variation-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:variation-lower">[eq:variation-lower]</a> satisfies $CN^{-2\alpha}<1$. Combining the upper and lower bounds and then taking late indices gives $$K_i^2\le\gamma_i(K_i+1)^2,\qquad\gamma_i\longrightarrow0.$$ This also proves boundedness if it was not known previously: $K_i/(K_i+1)\le\sqrt{\gamma_i}$, and, for $\sqrt{\gamma_i}<1$, one has $K_i\le\sqrt{\gamma_i}/(1-\sqrt{\gamma_i})\to0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
#### Small energy fixes the pointed root geometry

The vanishing energy is an integral statement. To contradict the prescribed change of scale, it remains to translate it into distance preservation on a single dense set of material points and then into preservation of the curvature-radius maximum.

<a id="stmt-final-ricci-envelope"></a>
<div id="final:ricci-envelope" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:final-ricci-bound"></a>
**Lemma 30** (A common time-dependent Ricci bound). *On a slightly smaller material neighborhood of the isolated cluster, for $0\le t\le1$, $$\label{eq:final-ricci-bound}
 |\mathop{\mathrm{Ric}}|(y,t)\le Q_*(t)\bigl[1+(a/l(y,t))^2\bigr],
 \qquad\|Q_*\|_{L^2([0,1])}\longrightarrow0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-final-ricci-envelope"><summary>Proof of stmt-final-ricci-envelope</summary>

*Proof.* Inside a fixed large multiple of $a$, use the ordinary local parabolic estimate underlying Proposition <a href="#prop:interior-ricci" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:interior-ricci">20</a> on a patch of scale a small fixed multiple of $\min(a,l(y,t))$. The enlarged energy balls contain these patches and their prior windows by the short-motion comparison. Since $\eta$ is bounded below on $[0,1]$, the estimate and Equation <a href="#eq:maximal-ricci" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:maximal-ricci">[eq:maximal-ricci]</a> give an envelope bounded by $C(H_*+\epsilon_i)$ times $1+(a/l)^2$. Its time $L^2$ norm tends to zero by Corollary <a href="#final:energy-vanishes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="final:energy-vanishes">29</a>.

On the exterior annular range, an envelope for the magnitude on all shells is obtained from a fixed outer shell and the sum of the positive first derivative suprema on the intervening dyadic shells. Indeed integrate $\nabla\mathop{\mathrm{Ric}}$ along the radial coordinate curves, using parallel transport for tensor norms. The controlled neck charts give length $O(r)$ on each shell, so each shell costs its scaled first derivative supremum. Norms on a fixed shell vary by the same bound along its angular paths. Dividing the estimates for $G=\eta\mathop{\mathrm{Ric}}$ by the fixed positive lower bound of $\eta$ is legitimate on this interval. Minkowski’s inequality and Proposition <a href="#prop:exterior-ricci" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:exterior-ricci">18</a> bound the time $L^2$ norm of that envelope by $$C\left[K+\widetilde\delta_i+
 \widetilde\delta_i\sum_{r\in[N_0a,r_0]}
                 \bigl((a/r)^\alpha+r^\alpha\bigr)\right]
 \longrightarrow0.$$ The outer-shell value tends to zero by the uncut Ricci improvement and its regular geometry. Only the positive-derivative errors are summed over all shells; the constant error in their zero-order estimate is not summed.

Outside this range but within the smaller isolated neighborhood, $l$ is bounded below in interval units. Otherwise choose a sequence with $l\to0$ there. If its images remain in a compact regular part of the original limiting space, smooth convergence is a contradiction. If they approach the isolated point, their distance from the cluster is bounded below in interval units and is much larger than $a$. At that distance scale the vanishing curvature radius and the root give two genuine tips, forcing a nonflat entry larger than $a$. The same argument applies when that distance tends to infinity in interval units but tends to zero originally, by rescaling with the distance itself. Proposition <a href="#prop:root-scale" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:root-scale">8</a> excludes this alternative. Equation <a href="#eq:ricci-improvement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ricci-improvement">[eq:ricci-improvement]</a> thus bounds Ricci in the remaining region by $C\epsilon_i$. Adding these three envelopes proves the lemma. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-final-good-points"></a>
<div id="final:good-points" class="lemma">

<a id="final:good-distance"></a>
**Lemma 31** (Good material points). *There is a single set of material points $\mathcal G_i$, independent of time, whose complement has volume $o(a_i^4)$, which is $o(a_i)$-dense at every $t\in[0,1]$ in a smaller neighborhood of the cluster. For points $x,y\in\mathcal G_i$ in that neighborhood whose distance at either endpoint is at most $Ca_i$, $$\label{final:good-distance}
 |d_1(x,y)-d_0(x,y)|=o(a_i),$$ uniformly for each fixed $C$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-final-good-points"><summary>Proof of stmt-final-good-points</summary>

*Proof.* Take nested material neighborhoods compactly contained in the region of Equation <a href="#eq:final-ricci-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:final-ricci-bound">[eq:final-ricci-bound]</a>. Uniform convergence of the original distances to $d_T$ gives a fixed positive original distance between the smaller neighborhood and the complement of the larger one. Thus any minimizing segment of interval length $O(a)$ with endpoints in the smaller neighborhood remains in the region where that equation applies.

<a id="final:distance-derivative"></a>
For such a segment $\gamma:[0,L]\to M$ parametrized by arclength, the geodesic curvature-radius estimate of Lemma <a href="#geom:geodesic-radius" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:geodesic-radius">9</a> and spatial $1$-Lipschitzness of $l$ imply, on its first half, $$l(\gamma(s))\ge \max\{l(x)-s,cs\}
                  \ge c'\bigl(l(x)+s\bigr).$$ The fixed cap in that geodesic estimate is irrelevant at these vanishing original lengths. The second half has the analogous bound from $y$. Integration gives <a id="final:geodesic-integral"></a>$$\label{final:geodesic-integral}
 \int_\gamma l^{-2}\,ds
          \le C\bigl(l(x,t)^{-1}+l(y,t)^{-1}\bigr).$$ The length variation formula under Ricci flow, applied to minimizing segments in both time directions, therefore yields, almost everywhere while $d_t(x,y)\le Ca$, $$\label{final:distance-derivative}
 a^{-1}|\partial_t d_t(x,y)|
 \le C_C Q_*(t)
         \bigl[1+a/l(x,t)+a/l(y,t)\bigr].$$ One may equivalently first use upper and lower one-sided length variations; for each fixed smooth flow the distance is locally Lipschitz in time, so these imply the almost-everywhere inequality.

<a id="final:endpoint-integral"></a>
The scale-sublevel covering in Theorem <a href="#thm:degeneration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:degeneration">6</a> gives $\mathop{\mathrm{Vol}}_t\{l<s\}\le Cs^4$ at these scales. Decomposing $\{l<a\}$ into the sets $2^{-k-1}a\le l<2^{-k}a$ proves $$\label{final:endpoint-integral}
 \int_{\{l<a\}}\frac a l\,d\mu_{g(t)}
 \le\sum_{k=0}^\infty 2^{k+1}C(2^{-k}a)^4
 \le Ca^4.$$ Fix the material measure $d\mu_0=d\mu_{g(0)}$. Since $\partial_t d\mu_g=-R\,d\mu_g$ and $|R|\le C\epsilon_i^2$, all these time-slice measures are uniformly comparable with $d\mu_0$. Put $q_i=\|Q_*\|_{L^1([0,1])}\to0$ and define $$F_i(x)=\int_0^1 Q_*(t)\frac a{l(x,t)}
                          \mathbf1_{\{l(x,t)<a\}}\,dt.$$ Fubini and Equation <a href="#final:endpoint-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="final:endpoint-integral">[final:endpoint-integral]</a> give $\int F_i\,d\mu_0\le Ca^4q_i$. For $q_i>0$, discard the material set $\mathcal B_i=\{F_i>\sqrt{q_i}\}$. Then $$\mu_0(\mathcal B_i)\le Ca^4\sqrt{q_i},\qquad
 \int_0^1 Q_*(t)(1+a/l(x,t))\,dt
                    \le2q_i+\sqrt{q_i}\quad(x\notin\mathcal B_i).$$ If $q_i=0$, take the good set to be the whole manifold, up to a null set, and the same conclusions hold. The lower ball-volume bound $\mathop{\mathrm{Vol}}_t B_t(x,r)\ge cr^4$ shows that every ball of radius $C'aq_i^{1/8}$ in the smaller neighborhood meets the good set when $C'$ is large enough. Indeed such a ball has greater volume than the entire bad set, uniformly in time. Taking the complement of $\mathcal B_i$ for $\mathcal G_i$ proves simultaneous $o(a)$ density.

For good endpoints, the time integral of the right side of Equation <a href="#final:distance-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="final:distance-derivative">[final:distance-derivative]</a> is $o(1)$ with a constant depending only on a fixed distance range. If the initial distance is at most $Ca$, apply that inequality up to the first time it reaches $(C+1)a$. Its integrated variation is smaller than $a/2$ for late indices, so such a first crossing cannot occur. The same argument backwards in time treats a pair initially specified at $t=1$. This proves Equation <a href="#final:good-distance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="final:good-distance">[final:good-distance]</a>, and also prevents points outside a larger bounded scaled ball from entering a smaller one during the comparison. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-final-root-fixed"></a>
<div id="final:root-fixed" class="proposition">

**Proposition 32** (The root maximum cannot change). *Let $\mathfrak a_i(t)$ denote the root scale of Proposition <a href="#prop:root-scale" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:root-scale">8</a>, computed in interval units. Then $$a_i^{-1}\bigl|\mathfrak a_i(1)-\mathfrak a_i(0)\bigr|
                      \longrightarrow0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-final-root-fixed"><summary>Proof of stmt-final-root-fixed</summary>

*Proof.* Choose a good material anchor within $o(a)$ of a maximizing point at time $0$. Extract pointed limits at the two endpoints with length unit $a$. Lemma <a href="#final:good-points" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="final:good-points">31</a> identifies these limits isometrically. Here is the metric argument in detail. On a fixed bounded ball choose a finite increasingly dense net of good material labels at time $0$. Their distance matrices at time $1$ differ by $o(1)$ in scaled units. The same is true of nets chosen at time $1$, and the first-crossing argument keeps every such label in a bounded ball at the other time. Apply compactness to their union and then diagonalize in the radius and the net mesh. The limiting correspondence preserves all pairwise distances and is dense and onto; completing it gives a pointed isometry. All bounded balls used here lie near the same isolated point of $Z$ in original units.

Every exceptional point of either limit is a genuine nontrivial orbifold point by Theorem <a href="#thm:degeneration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:degeneration">6</a>, so the regular part is determined by the metric space itself. The isometry is smooth there and preserves its Riemannian metric: in a sufficiently small regular convex neighborhood, squared distances to suitable nearby points give smooth coordinates with independent differentials; the distance-preserving map has the corresponding smooth coordinate expressions, as does its inverse. Smooth convergence on these regular regions is available at both endpoints. In particular the initial nonflat root patch persists in the other endpoint limit.

<a id="final:radius-convergence"></a>
We next check that the curvature radius itself, rather than only the distance metric, is preserved. For a regular point $z$ of the common nonflat limit $X$ define its stopped radius by <a id="final:stopped-radius"></a>$$\label{final:stopped-radius}
\begin{split}
 \lambda_X(z)=\sup\{r>0:\;&B_X(z,r)\text{ contains no exceptional point}\\
                       &\text{and }|\mathop{\mathrm{Rm}}_X|\le r^{-2}\text{ on }B_X(z,r)\}.
\end{split}$$ It is finite: a regular point of nonzero curvature exists at finite distance, so sufficiently large balls fail the curvature test. For any regular convergent sequence $z_i\to z$ at either endpoint, $$\label{final:radius-convergence}
                         l(z_i,t)/a_i\longrightarrow\lambda_X(z).$$ For the lower bound, take $r<s<\lambda_X(z)$ with $s$ admissible. The closed $r$-ball has compact regular geometry, and its curvature test has the strict margin $r^2|\mathop{\mathrm{Rm}}_X|\le(r/s)^2<1$. Smooth convergence and convergence of distances imply admissibility of every slightly smaller $r$-ball in the approximations. Let $r\uparrow\lambda_X(z)$. For the upper bound, take $r>\lambda_X(z)$ and then an intermediate radius between them. If its ball meets an exceptional point, genuine concentration approaches that point in the approximations and violates the $r$-curvature test. If it does not, its inadmissibility gives a regular point whose curvature strictly exceeds $r^{-2}$; this violation persists under smooth convergence. Thus the $r$-ball is eventually inadmissible. The original fixed cap for $l$, divided by the original length corresponding to $a_i$, tends to infinity, so it imposes no further restriction here. This proves Equation <a href="#final:radius-convergence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="final:radius-convergence">[final:radius-convergence]</a>, including possible equality cases at the limiting threshold.

The defining maximum is $$\frac{\mathfrak a_i(t)}{a_i}
 =\max_{y\in\mathcal U}
   \frac{l(y,t)}{a_i}
       \psi\bigl(l(y,t)^2|\mathop{\mathrm{Rm}}|(y,t)\bigr).$$ At a maximizer, $l/a_i$ is bounded below by a positive constant, because $\mathfrak a_i(t)/a_i$ is bounded below and $\psi$ is bounded. It is also bounded above by the detection and outermost-scale property of Proposition <a href="#prop:root-scale" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:root-scale">8</a>. Maximizers at time $1$ stay at bounded scaled distance from the common anchor. Indeed the persistent nonflat root patch supplies a nonflat entry of scale comparable to $a_i$ at that time; if a maximizing nonflat entry escaped from it, their distance-scale limit would have two genuine tips and produce an entry larger than the outermost scale. The same bounded-range statement at time $0$ follows from the choice of the anchor.

Consequently maximizers at either endpoint have subsequences converging to regular points of $X$. At any such point, Equation <a href="#final:radius-convergence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="final:radius-convergence">[final:radius-convergence]</a>, smooth curvature convergence, and continuity of $\psi$ show that their values tend to $$\lambda_X(z)\,
       \psi\bigl(\lambda_X(z)^2|\mathop{\mathrm{Rm}}_X|(z)\bigr).$$ That same regular point can be approximated in the other endpoint sequence, giving the same limit value there. A subsequence realizing any proposed strict difference between the two maxima is therefore impossible: its maximizing point at the larger end provides a competing point at the smaller end. Applying this in both directions proves the proposition. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-33"></a>
#### Smooth continuation on the original manifold

Proposition <a href="#final:root-fixed" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="final:root-fixed">32</a> contradicts the definite factor change of the comparable positive root scales at the endpoints of the intervals in Proposition <a href="#prop:buffered-intervals" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:buffered-intervals">12</a>. Hence a scalar-bounded flow cannot have a concentration point. Theorem <a href="#thm:degeneration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:degeneration">6</a> then gives a uniform full-curvature bound up to the original finite time $T$.

For completeness, this yields the precise extension required in Theorem <a href="#thm:extension" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:extension">1</a>. Curvature differentiation on a closed manifold gives bounds for every covariant derivative of curvature on any interval $[t_0,T)$ with $t_0>0$. Bounded Ricci curvature and $\partial_tg=-2\mathop{\mathrm{Ric}}$ first make $g(t)$ uniformly equivalent to $g(t_0)$. Integrating the connection variation and its successive derivatives, or working in the resulting fixed controlled charts, then gives uniform bounds and convergence for all coordinate derivatives of $g(t)$. Thus $g(t)$ converges smoothly to a positive definite smooth metric $g_T$ on the original manifold $M$. Short-time existence from $g_T$ follows from the Ricci–DeTurck equation on this same closed smooth manifold, followed by its diffeomorphism pullback. The flow equation determines all time derivatives from the spatial derivatives, so the old and new solutions join smoothly at $T$. Retaining the given flow for $t<T$ and this solution for $t\ge T$ gives a smooth Ricci flow $\widetilde g$ on $M\times[0,T+\varepsilon)$, for some $\varepsilon>0$, with $\widetilde g(t)=g(t)$ for every $t<T$. The argument uses arbitrary smooth initial data in real dimension four; no change of manifold or orbifold continuation is involved.

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "The outermost scale $a$, its smaller descendants, and the material collar at radius $b = Na$. An edge of logarithmic length $L$ changes the length unit by $e^{−2L}$. The collar extension preserves the inner geometry and supplies an ALE end. Incidence and scale are schematic.",
      "figure_id": "fig:scales",
      "pdf_page": 4,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:scales",
          "exact_quote": "The outermost scale \\(a\\), its smaller descendants, and the\n material collar at radius \\(b=Na\\). An edge of logarithmic length\n \\(L\\) changes the length unit by \\(e^{-2L}\\). The collar extension\n preserves the inner geometry and supplies an",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[\n  x=1cm,y=1cm,>=Stealth,\n  piece/.style={draw=blue!55!black,fill=blue!5,rounded corners=3pt,\n                minimum width=2.1cm,minimum height=.65cm,align=center},\n  edge/.style={draw=blue!55!black,thick},\n  every node/.style={font=\\small}]\n  \\node[piece] (root) at (0,0) {outermost piece\\\\scale $a$};\n  \\node[piece] (left) at (-2.6,-1.65) {descendant\\\\$a e^{-2L_1}$};\n  \\node[piece] (right) at (2.6,-1.65) {descendant\\\\$a e^{-2L_2}$};\n  \\node[piece] (deep) at (-2.6,-3.2)\n    {further descendant\\\\$a e^{-2L_1-2L_3}$};\n  \\draw[edge,->] (root) -- node[above left] {$L_1$} (left);\n  \\draw[edge,->] (root) -- node[above right] {$L_2$} (right);\n  \\draw[edge,->] (left) -- node[right] {$L_3$} (deep);\n  \\draw[edge] (root.north) -- (0,.9);\n  \\draw[draw=orange!75!black,thick] (-1.5,.9)--(1.5,.9);\n  \\node[right,align=left,text=orange!65!black] at (1.65,.9)\n    {fixed collar\\\\$b=Na$};\n  \\draw[edge,dashed,->] (0,.9)--(0,1.7);\n  \\node[above] at (0,1.7) {attached ALE end};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A tree diagram illustrating a bubble tree hierarchy:\n- Top node: Rounded rectangular box labeled \"outermost piece\" and \"scale $a$\".\n- An upward dashed arrow points from the top of the outermost piece to the label \"attached ALE end\".\n- A vertical bracket to the right of the outermost piece is labeled \"fixed collar\" and \"$b = Na$\".\n- Two downward arrows branch from the bottom of the outermost piece:\n  - Left arrow labeled \"$L_1$\" leads to a rounded rectangular node labeled \"descendant\" and \"$ae^{-2L_1}$\".\n  - Right arrow labeled \"$L_2$\" leads to a rounded rectangular node labeled \"descendant\" and \"$ae^{-2L_2}$\".\n- A downward arrow labeled \"$L_3$\" leads from the left descendant node to a rounded rectangular node labeled \"further descendant\" and \"$ae^{-2L_1-2L_3}$\"."
    },
    {
      "caption": "Parabolic normalization of a selected buffered interval. Write $j = j_{i}$ for the selected indices and $d_{i} = t_{ji} + 1 − t_{ji}$. The neighboring intervals each have duration at least $θd_{i}$; only the shortened buffers are drawn. The root scale is comparable to $a_{i}$ throughout the normalized interval, and its values at $0$ and $1$ differ by the fixed factor $e^{u}$ or $e^{−u}$. The diagram is schematic and does not assert monotonicity of the root scale.",
      "figure_id": "fig:buffered-intervals",
      "pdf_page": 24,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:buffered-intervals",
          "exact_quote": "Parabolic normalization of a selected buffered interval.\nWrite $j=j_i$ for the selected indices and\n$d_i=t_{j_i+1}-t_{j_i}$.  The neighboring intervals each have duration\nat least $\\theta d_i$; only the shortened buffers are drawn.\nThe root",
          "source_object": "fig:0002"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[\n  x=1cm,y=1cm,>=Stealth,\n  every node/.style={font=\\small},\n  central/.style={draw=blue!55!black,line width=1.2pt},\n  buffer/.style={draw=orange!75!black,line width=1.2pt}]\n  \\node[anchor=west] at (0,1.0) {physical time};\n  \\draw[buffer] (0,0)--(2,0);\n  \\draw[central] (2,0)--(9,0);\n  \\draw[buffer] (9,0)--(11,0);\n  \\draw[->] (11,0)--(11.7,0) node[right] {$t$};\n  \\foreach \\x in {0,2,9,11} \\draw (\\x,-.1)--(\\x,.1);\n  \\node[above] at (0,.13) {$t_{j_i}-\\theta d_i$};\n  \\node[above] at (2,.13) {$t_{j_i}$};\n  \\node[above] at (9,.13) {$t_{j_i+1}$};\n  \\node[above] at (11,.13) {$t_{j_i+1}+\\theta d_i$};\n  \\node[below,text=orange!65!black] at (1,-.13) {$\\theta d_i$};\n  \\node[below,text=blue!55!black] at (5.5,-.13) {$d_i$};\n  \\node[below,text=orange!65!black] at (10,-.13) {$\\theta d_i$};\n  \\draw[->] (5.5,-.65)--(5.5,-1.55)\n    node[midway,right=6pt] {$s=(t-t_{j_i})/d_i$};\n  \\node[anchor=west] at (0,-1.55) {interval units};\n  \\draw[buffer] (0,-2)--(2,-2);\n  \\draw[central] (2,-2)--(9,-2);\n  \\draw[buffer] (9,-2)--(11,-2);\n  \\draw[->] (11,-2)--(11.7,-2) node[right] {$s$};\n  \\foreach \\x in {0,2,9,11} \\draw (\\x,-2.1)--(\\x,-1.9);\n  \\node[below] at (0,-2.13) {$-\\theta$};\n  \\node[below] at (2,-2.13) {$0$};\n  \\node[below] at (9,-2.13) {$1$};\n  \\node[below] at (11,-2.13) {$1+\\theta$};\n  \\node at (5.5,-3.05)\n    {$g_i(s)=d_i^{-1}g(t_{j_i}+d_i s),\\qquad\n      \\epsilon_i=\\sqrt{d_i},\\qquad\n      a_i=\\dfrac{a(t_{j_i})}{\\epsilon_i}\\longrightarrow0$};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Two horizontal parallel timeline axes with a downward vertical arrow connecting them:\n- Top axis labeled \"physical time\", pointing rightwards with variable $t$:\n  - Four vertical tick marks labeled $t_{j_i} - \\theta d_i$, $t_{j_i}$, $t_{j_i+1}$, and $t_{j_i+1} + \\theta d_i$.\n  - Interval between $t_{j_i} - \\theta d_i$ and $t_{j_i}$ has a bracket labeled \"$\\theta d_i$\".\n  - Interval between $t_{j_i}$ and $t_{j_i+1}$ has a bracket labeled \"$d_i$\".\n  - Interval between $t_{j_i+1}$ and $t_{j_i+1} + \\theta d_i$ has a bracket labeled \"$\\theta d_i$\".\n- Downward vertical arrow connecting the axes labeled \"$s = (t - t_{j_i})/d_i$\".\n- Bottom axis labeled \"interval units\", pointing rightwards with variable $s$:\n  - Four vertical tick marks labeled $-\\theta$, $0$, $1$, and $1 + \\theta$.\n- Below the bottom axis are mathematical expressions:\n  $g_i(s) = d_i^{-1}g(t_{j_i} + d_is)$, $\\quad \\epsilon_i = \\sqrt{d_i}$, $\\quad a_i = \\frac{a(t_{j_i})}{\\epsilon_i} \\longrightarrow 0$."
    }
  ],
